), we begin with a table of contents to re-orient the reader.

Appendix A
Baselines

A.1
The mean-field approximation
Y_{\mathrm{mfa}}

Following
18
;
52
;
2
, we define the mean-field analytic approximation by assuming that neurons in the same hidden layer are independent:

\displaystyle Y_{\mathrm{mfa}}

\displaystyle=Y^{\ell}

\displaystyle Y^{k}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\mu^{k},\Sigma^{k})

\displaystyle\forall k

\displaystyle\in\mathinner{\left\{1\ldots\ell\right\}}

\displaystyle\mu^{k}

\displaystyle=\expect g(Y^{k-1};A^{k},b^{k},C^{k},d^{k})

\displaystyle\Sigma^{k}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathinner{\left[\Cov g(Y^{k-1};A^{k},b^{k},C^{k},d^{k})\right]}_{ij},&i=j\\
0,&\text{else}\end{cases}

(mean-field assumption)

\displaystyle Y^{0}

\displaystyle=X

A.2
The linear approximation
Y_{\mathrm{lin}}

Following
48
;
33
;
40
;
26
, we define the linear approximation as the asymptotically Normal output distribution (pursuant to the delta method) in the limit of small input variance:

\displaystyle Y_{\mathrm{lin}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(f(\expect X),\nabla f(\expect X)\Cov X\nabla f(\expect X)^{\intercal})

A.3
The unscented approximation(s)
Y_{\mathrm{u95}}
and
Y_{\mathrm{u02}}

An unscented transformation is a quadrature rule for approximating a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
2n+1
point masses whose locations (called sigma points) and weights satisfy certain first- and second-order moment matching conditions.
This technique was developed to improve upon linearization in nonlinear Kalman filtering
(
22
;
25
;
21
;
24
;
53
;
23
)
.

There are (at least) two unscented transformations: a one-parameter family, which we refer to as Unscented’95; and a three-parameter family, which we refer to as Unscented’02.

•

Unscented’95
(
22
;
25
;
21
)
, used in
3
;
1
, is a one-parameter family of unscented transformations, parameterized by a single shape hyperparameter
\kappa
.

•

Unscented’02
(
24
;
53
)
is a three-parameter family of unscented transformations, parameterized by three shape hyperparameters
(\alpha,\beta,\kappa)
.
This version, with default values tuned to be drastically more localized in
X
-space than Unscented’95, is usually called “the” unscented transformation in current filtering research
(
20
)
and tooling
(
30
)
.

Appendix B
Preliminaries: Gaussian integrals

Definition B.1
.

The bivariate normal CDF
\Phi_{2}
is defined by

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(h,k;\rho)

\displaystyle=\probability\mathinner{\left[Z_{1}\leq h,Z_{2}\leq k\right]},

where

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}Z_{1}\\
Z_{2}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\mathinner{\left(\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&\rho\\
\rho&1\end{pmatrix}\right)}.

Remark B.2
.

While we consider it a valid “closed form” atom, the bivariate normal CDF can be a difficult transcendental function
to evaluate.
Many software packages such as SciPy
(
52
)

implement the multivariate normal CDF by a (quasi-) Monte Carlo
integration over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is too expensive for our purposes.
Furthermore, we frequently use the expression
\Phi(h,k;\rho)-\Phi(h,k;0)
, which is
vulnerable to cancellation error for extreme values of
h
,

k
, and
\rho
.
To avoid this, we use 10-point Gaussian quadrature
of the one-dimensional proper integral

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}(h,k;\rho)-\Phi_{2}(h,k;0)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}^{\prime}\Phi_{2}(h,k;\rho^{\prime})\dif\rho^{\prime},

using

\displaystyle\partial_{\rho}\Phi_{2}(h,k;\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2(1-\rho^{2})}\mathinner{\left(h^{2}+k^{2}-2\rho hk\right)}},

a helpful identity found in
11
.

Definition B.3
.

The bivariate Normal density is

\displaystyle\phi_{2}(h,k;\rho)

\displaystyle=\mathinner{\dfrac{\partial{{}^{2}}}{\partial{h}\partial{k}}}\Phi_{2}(h,k;\rho).

Definition B.4
.

The partial derivative of the joint CDF is

\displaystyle\Phi_{2;1}(h,k;\rho)=\mathinner{\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{h}}}\Phi_{2}(h,k;\rho)

(4)

Lemma B.5
.

The function
\Phi_{2;1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Phi_{2;1}(h,k;\rho)

\displaystyle=\phi(h)\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{k-\rho h}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}}\right)}.

Proof.

Letting
Z_{1},Z_{2}
be standard normal with correlation
\rho
, there is a probabilistic interpretation of
\Phi_{2;1}
:

\displaystyle\Phi_{2;1}(h,k;\rho)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{f_{Z_{1}}(h)}_{\text{marginal density}}\underbrace{\probability\mathinner{\left(Z_{2}\leq k\mid Z_{1}=h\right)}}_{\text{conditional cdf}}

\displaystyle=\phi(h)\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{k-\rho h}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}}\right)}.

∎

Lemma B.6

(Multivariate Stein’s lemma)
.

Let
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
be a multivariate normal random vector.
Then

\displaystyle\expect(X_{1}-\expect X_{1})f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Cov(X_{1},X_{i})\expect\mathinner{\left[\partial_{X_{i}}f(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\right]}

Lemma B.7

(Stein simplification of
L_{\sigma}
)
.

Let
L_{\sigma}
be defined as in Definition
2.3
, and suppose that
\sigma
is differentiable.
Then

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}\expect\sigma^{\prime}(Z_{1}),

\displaystyle Z_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\nu_{11}).

Proof.

Straightforward application of Lemma
B.6
.
∎

Lemma B.8

(Univariate integrals)
.

If
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\nu)
, then

\displaystyle\expect\Phi(Z)

\displaystyle=\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}

(5a)

\displaystyle\expect\phi(Z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}

(5b)

\displaystyle\expect Z\Phi(Z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1+\nu)^{3/2}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}

(5c)

Proof of (
5a
).

Introducing an independent
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\expect\Phi(Z)

\displaystyle=\expect\probability\mathinner{\left[{Z\leq X}\mid X\right]}

(6)

\displaystyle=\probability\mathinner{\left[{Z\leq X}\right]}

(by the law of total probability)

\displaystyle=\probability\mathinner{\left[Z-X\leq 0\right]}

(7)

We conclude by noting that the random variable
Z-X
has a
Normal distribution with mean
-\mu
and variance
1+\nu
.
∎

Proof of (
5b
).

We use
\phi=\Phi^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\expect\phi(Z)

\displaystyle=\expect\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\dif{}}{\dif{t}}}\right|_{t=0}\Phi(Z+t)

(8)

\displaystyle=\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\dif{}}{\dif{t}}}\right|_{t=0}\expect\Phi(Z+t)

(dominated convergence theorem)

\displaystyle=\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\dif{}}{\dif{t}}}\right|_{t=0}\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu+t}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}

(by (
5a
))

∎

Proof of (
5c
).

We use the Gaussian ODE

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(x)+x\phi(x)

\displaystyle=0.

(9)

Centering and applying Lemma
B.6
,

\displaystyle\expect Z\phi(Z)

\displaystyle=\expect(Z-\mu)\phi(Z)+\mu\expect\phi(Z)

(centering)

\displaystyle=\nu\expect\phi^{\prime}(Z)+\mu\expect\phi(Z)

(Stein’s)

\displaystyle=-\nu\expect Z\Phi(Z)+\mu\expect\phi(Z)

(Gaussian ODE)

Collecting like terms and solving for (I),

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left(1+\nu\right)}\expect Z\Phi(Z)

\displaystyle=\mu\expect\phi(Z)

(10)

\displaystyle\expect Z\Phi(Z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu}{1+\nu}\expect\phi(Z)

(11)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu_{1}}{(1+\nu_{11})^{3/2}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\right)}

(by (
5b
))

∎

Lemma B.9

(Bivariate integrals)
.

Let

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}\\
X_{2}\end{pmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}\mathinner{\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{11}&\nu_{12}\\
\nu_{12}&\nu_{22}\end{pmatrix}\right)}.

Then

\displaystyle\expect\Phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}

(12a)

\displaystyle\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}

(12b)

\displaystyle\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}

(12c)

\displaystyle\expect\phi^{\prime}(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{-\mu_{1}}{1+\nu_{11}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})+\frac{-\nu_{12}}{1+\nu_{11}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})

(12d)

Proof of (
12a
).

Introduce independent

Z_{1},Z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
Then

\displaystyle\expect\Phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\expect\probability\mathinner{\left[Z_{1}\leq X_{1}\mid X_{1}\right]}\probability\mathinner{\left[Z_{2}\leq X_{2}\mid X_{2}\right]}

(13)

\displaystyle=\expect\probability\mathinner{\left[Z_{1}\leq X_{1},Z_{2}\leq X_{2}\mid X_{1},X_{2}\right]}

(independence)

\displaystyle=\probability\mathinner{\left[Z_{1}\leq X_{1},Z_{2}\leq X_{2}\right]}

(by the law of total probability)

\displaystyle=\probability\mathinner{\left[Z_{1}-X_{1}\leq 0,Z_{2}-X_{2}\leq 0\right]}.

(14)

We conclude by using the fact that
(Z_{1}-X_{1},Z_{2}-X_{2})
is
jointly Normal with distribution

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}Z_{1}-X_{1}\\
Z_{2}-X_{2}\end{pmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}\mathinner{\left(\begin{pmatrix}-\mu_{1}\\
-\mu_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1+\nu_{11}&\nu_{12}\\
\nu_{12}&1+\nu_{22}\end{pmatrix}\right)}.

(15)

∎

Proof of (
12b
).

Using
\phi=\Phi^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\expect\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\dif{}}{\dif{t}}}\right|_{t=0}\Phi(X_{1}+t)\Phi(X_{2})

(16)

\displaystyle=\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\dif{}}{\dif{t}}}\right|_{t=0}\expect\Phi(X_{1}+t)\Phi(X_{2})

(dominated convergence theorem)

\displaystyle=\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\dif{}}{\dif{t}}}\right|_{t=0}\probability\mathinner{\left(Z_{1}-X_{1}-t\leq 0,Z_{2}-X_{2}\leq 0\right)}

(introducing
Z_{1},Z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
)

\displaystyle=\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\dif{}}{\dif{t}}}\right|_{t=0}\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}+t}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}

(17)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}

(18)

∎

Proof of (
12c
).

Using
\phi=\Phi^{\prime}
in both terms,

\displaystyle\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\expect\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\partial{{}^{2}}}{\partial{t}\partial{s}}}\right|_{t=0,s=0}\Phi(X_{1}+t)\Phi(X_{2}+s)

(19)

\displaystyle=\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\partial{{}^{2}}}{\partial{t}\partial{s}}}\right|_{t=0,s=0}\expect\Phi(X_{1}+t)\Phi(X_{2}+s)

(dominated convergence theorem)

\displaystyle=\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\partial{{}^{2}}}{\partial{t}\partial{s}}}\right|_{t=0,s=0}\probability\mathinner{\left(Z_{1}-X_{1}-t\leq 0,Z_{2}-X_{2}-s\leq 0\right)}

(introducing
Z_{1},Z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
)

\displaystyle=\left.\mathinner{\dfrac{\partial{{}^{2}}}{\partial{t}\partial{s}}}\right|_{t=0,s=0}\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}+t}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}+s}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}

(20)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}\,\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}}\phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}

(pdf is derivative of cdf)

∎

Proof of (
12d
).

We use the Gaussian ODE

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(x)+x\phi(x)

\displaystyle=0.

(21)

Using this fact,

\displaystyle\expect\phi^{\prime}(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=-\expect X_{1}\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

(22)

\displaystyle=-\mu_{1}\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})-\expect(X_{1}-\mu_{1})\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

(centering)

\displaystyle=-\mu_{1}\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})-\nu_{11}\underbrace{\expect\phi^{\prime}(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})}_{\text{same as LHS}}-\nu_{12}\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})

(Lemma
B.6
)

Collecting like terms and solving for
\expect\phi^{\prime}(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})
,,

\displaystyle\expect\phi^{\prime}(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{-\mu_{1}}{1+\nu_{11}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})+\frac{-\nu_{12}}{1+\nu_{11}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2}).

(23)

∎

Appendix C
Derivation of uncertainty propagation formulas for probit activation

In this appendix, we derive the
M_{\sigma}
,
K_{\sigma}
, and

L_{\sigma}
functions (Def.
2.3
) for the normal
CDF activation function

\displaystyle\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{0}^{x}e^{-\frac{1}{2}u^{2}}du=2\Phi(x)-1,\quad\Phi(x)=\probability_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}(Z\leq x)

(24)

Lemma C.1
.

Let
M_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
24
).

\displaystyle M_{\sigma}(\mu;\nu)

\displaystyle=\sigma\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}

Proof.

Let
X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\nu)
.

\displaystyle\expect\sigma(X)

\displaystyle=-1+2\expect\Phi\mathinner{\left(X\right)}

(25)

This is a direct application of Lemma
B.8
, (
5a
).
∎

Lemma C.2
.

Let
K_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
24
).

\displaystyle K(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=4\left.\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\rho^{\prime}\right)}\right|^{\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}}_{\rho^{\prime}=0},

where
\Phi_{2}
is the bivariate normal CDF.

Proof.

The covariance may be expressed as

\displaystyle\Cov(\sigma(X_{1}),\sigma(X_{2}))

\displaystyle=4\Cov(\Phi(X_{1}),\Phi(X_{2}))

(26)

\displaystyle=4\expect\Phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})-4\expect\Phi(X_{1})\expect\Phi(X_{2})

(27)

Now apply Lemma
B.9
to the first term.
∎

Lemma C.3
.

Let
L_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
24
).
Then

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=2\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\right)}.

Proof.

Using Lemma
B.7
, we have

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}\expect\sigma^{\prime}(X_{1})

(28)

\displaystyle=2\nu_{12}\expect\phi(X_{1})

(29)

where
\phi=\Phi^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
B.8
, applied to
X_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\nu_{11})
,

\displaystyle\expect\phi(X_{1})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\right)}.

(30)

∎

Appendix D
Derivation of uncertainty propagation formulas for GeLU activation

In this appendix, we derive the
M_{\sigma}
,
K_{\sigma}
and

L_{\sigma}
functions (Def.
2.3
) for the GeLU activation function

\displaystyle\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=x\Phi(x).

(31)

Lemma D.1
.

Let
M_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
31
).
Then

\displaystyle M_{\sigma}(\mu;\nu)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}+\mu\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}.

Proof.

Let
X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\nu)
and
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
Centering
X
,

\displaystyle M_{\sigma}(\mu;\nu)=\expect X\Phi(X)

\displaystyle=\expect(X-\mu)\Phi(X)+\mu\expect\Phi(X)

\displaystyle=\expect(X-\mu)\Phi(X)+\mu\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}

(using Lemma
B.8
, (
5a
))

\displaystyle=\nu\expect\phi(X)+\mu\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}

(using Lemma
B.6
)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}+\mu\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+\nu}}\right)}

(using Lemma
B.8
, (
5b
))

∎

Lemma D.2
.

Let
K_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
31
).
Then

K_{\sigma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})\\
=\mathinner{\left(\mu_{1}\nu_{12}+\mu_{2}\nu_{11}-\frac{\mu_{1}\nu_{12}\nu_{11}}{1+\nu_{11}}\right)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}\\
+\mathinner{\left(\mu_{2}\nu_{12}+\mu_{1}\nu_{22}-\frac{\mu_{2}\nu_{12}\nu_{22}}{1+\nu_{22}}\right)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}},\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}\\
+\frac{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}+\nu_{12}^{2}\mathinner{\left(1-\frac{\nu_{11}}{1+\nu_{11}}-\frac{\nu_{22}}{1+\nu_{22}}\right)}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}\\
+\mathinner{\left(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}+\nu_{12}\right)}\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}\\
-M_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11})M_{\sigma}(\mu_{2};\nu_{22})

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}\\
X_{2}\end{pmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{11}&\nu_{12}\\
\nu_{12}&\nu_{22}\end{pmatrix}\right).

(32)

Our strategy is to use the formula

\displaystyle\Cov(\sigma(X_{1}),\sigma(X_{2}))=\expect\sigma(X_{1})\sigma(X_{2})-\expect\sigma(X_{1})\expect\sigma(X_{2})

and use Lemma
D.1
for the second term.
For the cross term, we first center the random variables:

\expect X_{1}\Phi(X_{1})X_{2}\Phi(X_{2})=\underbrace{\expect(X_{1}-\mu_{1})\Phi(X_{1})(X_{2}-\mu_{2})\Phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(I)}}\\
+\underbrace{\expect\mu_{1}\Phi(X_{1})(X_{2}-\mu_{2})\Phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(II)}}\\
+\underbrace{\expect(X_{1}-\mu_{1})\Phi(X_{1})\mu_{2}\Phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(III)}}\\
+\underbrace{\expect\mu_{1}\Phi(X_{1})\mu_{2}\Phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(IV)}}

(33)

For term (I) of (
33
),

(I)

\displaystyle=\expect(X_{1}-\mu_{1})\Phi(X_{1})(X_{2}-\mu_{2})\Phi(X_{2})

(34)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\Cov(X_{1},X_{i})\expect\partial_{X_{i}}\mathinner{\left(\Phi(X_{1})(X_{2}-\mu_{2})\Phi(X_{2})\right)}

(Lemma
B.6
)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\nu_{11}\expect\phi(X_{1})(X_{2}-\mu_{2})\Phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(I.a)}}+\nu_{12}\expect\Phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})+\underbrace{\nu_{12}\expect\Phi(X_{1})(X_{2}-\mu_{2})\phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(I.b)}}

(35)

We apply Lemma
B.6
to (I.a) and (I.b) to get

\displaystyle\text{(I.a)}+\text{(I.b)}

\displaystyle=\nu_{11}\expect\phi(X_{1})(X_{2}-\mu_{2})\Phi(X_{2})+\nu_{12}\expect\Phi(X_{1})(X_{2}-\mu_{2})\phi(X_{2})

(36)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\nu_{11}\expect\mathinner{\left[\nu_{12}\phi^{\prime}(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})+\nu_{22}\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})\right]}\\
&\quad+\nu_{12}\expect\mathinner{\left[\nu_{12}\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})+\nu_{22}\Phi(X_{1})\phi^{\prime}(X_{2})\right]}\end{split}

(37)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\nu_{12}\nu_{11}\underbrace{\expect\phi^{\prime}(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(I.c)}}+\nu_{12}\nu_{22}\underbrace{\expect\Phi(X_{1})\phi^{\prime}(X_{2})}_{\text{(I.d)}}\\
&\quad+\mathinner{\left(\nu_{11}\nu_{22}+\nu_{12}^{2}\right)}\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})\end{split}

(38)

By Lemma
B.9
, (
12d
), we have

(I.c)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\mu_{1}}{1+\nu_{11}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})+\frac{-\nu_{12}}{1+\nu_{11}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})

(I.d)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\mu_{2}}{1+\nu_{22}}\expect\Phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})+\frac{-\nu_{12}}{1+\nu_{22}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})

Combining these last two equations with (
35
) and (
38
), (I) becomes

\text{(I)}=\nu_{12}\nu_{11}\mathinner{\left(\frac{-\mu_{1}}{1+\nu_{11}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})+\frac{-\nu_{12}}{1+\nu_{11}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})\right)}\\
+\nu_{12}\nu_{22}\mathinner{\left(\frac{-\mu_{2}}{1+\nu_{22}}\expect\Phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})+\frac{-\nu_{12}}{1+\nu_{22}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})\right)}\\
+\mathinner{\left(\nu_{11}\nu_{22}+\nu_{12}^{2}\right)}\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})\\
+\nu_{12}\expect\Phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

(39)

which simplifies to

\text{(I)}=\frac{-\mu_{1}\nu_{12}\nu_{11}}{1+\nu_{11}}\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})+\frac{-\mu_{2}\nu_{12}\nu_{22}}{1+\nu_{22}}\expect\Phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})\\
+\mathinner{\left[\nu_{11}\nu_{22}+\nu_{12}^{2}\mathinner{\left(1-\frac{\nu_{11}}{1+\nu_{11}}-\frac{\nu_{22}}{1+\nu_{22}}\right)}\right]}\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})\\
+\nu_{12}\expect\Phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})

(40)

For terms (II-III) of (
33
), we apply Lemma
B.6
to get

\displaystyle\text{(II)}=\expect\mu_{1}\Phi(X_{1})(X_{2}-\mu_{2})\Phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}\mathinner{\left(\nu_{12}\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})+\nu_{22}\expect\Phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})\right)}

\displaystyle\text{(III)}=\expect(X_{1}-\mu_{1})\Phi(X_{1})\mu_{2}\Phi(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\mu_{2}\mathinner{\left(\nu_{11}\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})+\nu_{12}\expect\Phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})\right)}

Now (
33
) becomes:

\expect X_{1}\Phi(X_{1})X_{2}\Phi(X_{2})\\
=\mathinner{\left(\mu_{1}\nu_{12}+\mu_{2}\nu_{11}-\frac{\mu_{1}\nu_{12}\nu_{11}}{1+\nu_{11}}\right)}\underbrace{\expect\phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(V.a)}}\\
+\mathinner{\left(\mu_{2}\nu_{12}+\mu_{1}\nu_{22}-\frac{\mu_{2}\nu_{12}\nu_{22}}{1+\nu_{22}}\right)}\underbrace{\expect\Phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(V.b)}}\\
+\mathinner{\left[\nu_{11}\nu_{22}+\nu_{12}^{2}\mathinner{\left(1-\frac{\nu_{11}}{1+\nu_{11}}-\frac{\nu_{22}}{1+\nu_{22}}\right)}\right]}\underbrace{\expect\phi(X_{1})\phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(V.c)}}\\
+\mathinner{\left(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}+\nu_{12}\right)}\underbrace{\expect\Phi(X_{1})\Phi(X_{2})}_{\text{(V.d)}}

(41)

By Lemma
B.9
, (
12b
),

(V.a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}

(42)

and

(V.b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}},\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}.

(43)

By Lemma
B.9
, (
12c
),

(V.c)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}\,\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}}\phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}.

(44)

By Lemma
B.9
, (
12a
),

(V.d)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}

(45)

The final form of (
41
) is

\expect X_{1}\Phi(X_{1})X_{2}\Phi(X_{2})\\
=\mathinner{\left(\mu_{1}\nu_{12}+\mu_{2}\nu_{11}-\frac{\mu_{1}\nu_{12}\nu_{11}}{1+\nu_{11}}\right)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}\\
+\mathinner{\left(\mu_{2}\nu_{12}+\mu_{1}\nu_{22}-\frac{\mu_{2}\nu_{12}\nu_{22}}{1+\nu_{22}}\right)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}},\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}\\
+\frac{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}+\nu_{12}^{2}\mathinner{\left(1-\frac{\nu_{11}}{1+\nu_{11}}-\frac{\nu_{22}}{1+\nu_{22}}\right)}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}\\
+\mathinner{\left(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}+\nu_{12}\right)}\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\nu_{11})(1+\nu_{22})}}\right)}

(46)

The conclusion follows from subtracting
M(\mu_{1};\nu_{11})M(\mu_{2};\nu_{22})
.
∎

Lemma D.3
.

Let
L_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
31
).
Then

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}\frac{\mu_{1}}{(1+\nu_{11})^{3/2}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\right)}+\nu_{12}\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\right)}.

(47)

Proof.

By Lemma
B.7
,

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}\expect\sigma^{\prime}(X_{1}),

\displaystyle X_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\nu_{11})

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}\underbrace{\expect X_{1}\phi(X_{1})}_{\text{(I)}}+\nu_{12}\underbrace{\expect\Phi(X_{1})}_{\text{(II)}}

(product rule)

By Lemma
B.8
, (
5c
)

(I)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu_{1}}{(1+\nu_{11})^{3/2}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\right)}.

(48)

Term (II) is given by

\displaystyle\text{(II)}=\expect\Phi(X_{1})

\displaystyle=\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\right)}

(49)

Applying terms (I)–(II),

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}\frac{\mu_{1}}{(1+\nu_{11})^{3/2}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\right)}+\nu_{12}\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{11}}}\right)}

(50)

∎

Appendix E
Derivation of uncertainty propagation formulas for ReLU activation

In this appendix, we derive the uncertainty propagation formulas for ReLU activation

\displaystyle\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=\max(0,x)

(51)

using the identity

\displaystyle\operatorname{ReLU}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\alpha^{-1}\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha x).

(52)

See
32
for another way to derive
M
and
K
.

Lemma E.1
.

Let
M_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
51
).
Then

\displaystyle M_{\sigma}(\mu;\nu)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\nu}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{\nu}}\right)}+\mu\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{\nu}}\right)}.

Proof.

By the dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle M_{\sigma}(\mu;\nu)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\alpha^{-1}\expect M_{\operatorname{GeLU}}(\alpha\mu;\alpha^{2}\nu)

(53)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\alpha^{-1}\mathinner{\left[\frac{\alpha^{2}\nu}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\alpha\mu}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu}}\right)}+\mu\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\alpha\mu}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu}}\right)}\right]}

(by Lemma
D.1
)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\nu}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{\nu}}\right)}+\mu\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{\nu}}\right)}

(54)

∎

Lemma E.2
.

Let
K_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
51
).
Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}K_{\sigma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})&=\mu_{2}\sqrt{\nu_{11}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}\right)}\\
&\quad+\mu_{1}\sqrt{\nu_{22}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{22}}},\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}\right)}\\
&\quad+\mathinner{\left(\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}-\frac{\nu_{12}^{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}\right)}\phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}\right)}\\
&\quad+\mathinner{\left(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}+\nu_{12}\right)}\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}\right)}\\
&\quad-M_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11})M_{\sigma}(\mu_{2};\nu_{22})\end{split}

Proof.

By the dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle\expect\sigma(X_{1})\sigma(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\expect\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\alpha^{-2}\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha X_{1})\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha X_{2})

(55)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\alpha^{-2}\expect\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha X_{1})\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha X_{2})

(56)

To compute
\alpha^{-2}\expect\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha X_{1})\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha X_{2})
, we use (
46
) (Proof of Lemma
D.2
) while scaling
\mu
s by
\alpha
and
\nu
s by
\alpha^{2}
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\alpha^{-2}\expect\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha X_{1})\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha X_{2})\\
&=\frac{\alpha\mu_{1}\nu_{12}+\alpha\mu_{2}\nu_{11}-\alpha^{3}\frac{\mu_{1}\nu_{12}\nu_{11}}{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}}}\\
&\qquad\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\alpha\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}}},\frac{\alpha\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22}}};\frac{\alpha^{2}\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11})(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22})}}\right)}\\
&\quad+\frac{\alpha\mu_{2}\nu_{12}+\alpha\mu_{1}\nu_{22}-\alpha^{3}\frac{\mu_{2}\nu_{12}\nu_{22}}{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22}}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22}}}\\
&\qquad\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\alpha\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22}}},\frac{\alpha\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}}};\frac{\alpha^{2}\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11})(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22})}}\right)}\\
&\quad+\frac{\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}\nu_{22}+\alpha^{2}\nu_{12}^{2}\mathinner{\left(1-\frac{\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}}{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}}-\frac{\alpha^{2}\nu_{22}}{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22}}\right)}}{\sqrt{(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11})(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22})}}\\
&\qquad\phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\alpha\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}}},\frac{\alpha\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22}}};\frac{\alpha^{2}\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11})(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22})}}\right)}\\
&\quad+\mathinner{\left(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}+\nu_{12}\right)}\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\alpha\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}}},\frac{\alpha\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22}}};\frac{\alpha^{2}\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11})(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{22})}}\right)}\end{split}

(57)

Taking
\alpha\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\expect\sigma(X_{1})\sigma(X_{2})&=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\alpha^{-2}\expect\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha X_{1})\operatorname{GeLU}(\alpha X_{2})\\
&=\mu_{2}\sqrt{\nu_{11}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}\right)}\\
&\quad+\mu_{1}\sqrt{\nu_{22}}\Phi_{2;1}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{22}}},\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}\right)}\\
&\quad+\mathinner{\left(\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}-\frac{\nu_{12}^{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}\right)}\phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}\right)}\\
&\quad+\mathinner{\left(\mu_{1}\mu_{2}+\nu_{12}\right)}\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{22}}};\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}\right)}\end{split}

(58)

The conclusion follows after subtracting
\expect\sigma(X_{1})\expect\sigma(X_{2})
.
∎

Lemma E.3
.

Let
L_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
51
).
Then

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}}\right)}.

Proof.

By the dominated convergence theorem,

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\alpha^{-2}L_{\operatorname{GeLU}}(\alpha\mu_{1};\alpha^{2}\nu_{11},\alpha^{2}\nu_{22},\alpha^{2}\nu_{12})

(59)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\alpha^{-2}\mathinner{\left[\alpha^{2}\nu_{12}\frac{\alpha\mu_{1}}{(1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11})^{3/2}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\alpha\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}}}\right)}+\alpha^{2}\nu_{12}\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\alpha\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\alpha^{2}\nu_{11}}}\right)}\right]}

(by Lemma
D.3
)

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}}\right)}

(60)

∎

Appendix F
Derivation of uncertainty propagation formulas for Heaviside activation

In this appendix, we state the
M_{\sigma}
,
K_{\sigma}
and

L_{\sigma}
functions (Def.
2.3
) for the Heaviside activation function

\displaystyle\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=\bm{1}_{x>0}

(61)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\mathinner{\left(1/2+1/2\hat{\sigma}(\alpha x)\right)}

(62)

where
\hat{\sigma}
is the odd probit sigmoid defined as in (
51
).
The idea of the proofs is to take dominated limits of the results in Appendix.
C
, similar to Appendix
E
’s treatment of Appendix
D
, so we omit them.

Lemma F.1
.

Let
\sigma
be the Heaviside function as in (
61
).
Then the functions defined in Def.
2.3
are

\displaystyle M_{\sigma}(\mu;\nu)

\displaystyle=\Phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{\nu}}\right)},

\displaystyle K_{\sigma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\left.\Phi_{2}\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}},\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{\nu_{22}}};\rho^{\prime}\right)}\right|_{\rho^{\prime}=0}^{\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}\nu_{22}}}},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu_{12}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}}\phi\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{\nu_{11}}}\right)}.

Appendix G
Derivation of uncertainty propagation formulas for sine activation

In this appendix, we derive the
M_{\sigma}
,
K_{\sigma}
and

L_{\sigma}
functions (Def.
2.3
) for the sinusoidal activation function

\displaystyle\sigma(x)

\displaystyle=\sin(x).

(63)

We begin by recalling the identities that if
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\nu)
,

\displaystyle\expect\sin(Z)

\displaystyle=e^{-\nu/2}\sin(\mu)

(64)

\displaystyle\expect\cos(Z)

\displaystyle=e^{-\nu/2}\cos(\mu)

(65)

These formulas, which can be derived from the characteristic function of the normal distribution, allow moments of
\sin(Z)
and
\cos(Z)
to be computed exactly.
There is no need, as in
45
to use analytic approximations in this step.

From (
64
) immediately follows:

Lemma G.1
.

Let
M_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
63
).
Then

\displaystyle M_{\sigma}(\mu;\nu)

\displaystyle=e^{-\nu/2}\sin(\mu).

Lemma G.2
.

Let
K_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
63
).
Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}K_{\sigma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})&=\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\left[e^{\nu^{*}+\nu_{12}}-e^{\nu^{*}}\right]}\cos(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\left[e^{\nu^{*}-\nu_{12}}-e^{\nu^{*}}\right]}\cos(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}),\end{split}

where

\displaystyle\nu^{*}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\nu_{11}+\nu_{22}}{2}.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}\\
X_{2}\end{pmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{11}&\nu_{12}\\
\nu_{12}&\nu_{22}\end{pmatrix}\right).

(66)

Then by inserting (
63
), we have

\displaystyle\Cov(\sigma(X_{1}),\sigma(X_{2}))

\displaystyle=\expect\sin(X_{1})\sin(X_{2})-\expect\sin(X_{1})\expect\sin(X_{2}).

(67)

Using some trigonometric identities and (
65
), the first term of (
67
) becomes

\displaystyle\expect\sin(X_{1})\sin(X_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\expect\cos(\underbrace{X_{1}-X_{2}}_{\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2},\nu_{11}+\nu_{22}-2\nu_{12})})-\frac{1}{2}\expect\cos(\underbrace{X_{1}+X_{2}}_{\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},\nu_{11}+\nu_{22}+2\nu_{12})})

(68)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\frac{1}{2}\exp\mathinner{\left(-\frac{\nu_{11}+\nu_{22}}{2}+\nu_{12}\right)}\cos(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{2}\exp\mathinner{\left(-\frac{\nu_{11}+\nu_{22}}{2}-\nu_{12}\right)}\cos(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})\end{split}

(69)

The second term of (
67
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\expect\sin(X_{1})\expect\sin(X_{2})&=\frac{1}{2}\exp\mathinner{\left(-\frac{\nu_{11}+\nu_{22}}{2}\right)}\cos(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{2}\exp\mathinner{\left(-\frac{\nu_{11}+\nu_{22}}{2}\right)}\cos(\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})\end{split}

(70)

The result follows from collecting like terms.
∎

Lemma G.3
.

Let
L_{\sigma}
be defined as in Def.
2.3
, with

\sigma
as in (
63
).
Then

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}e^{-\nu_{11}/2}\cos(\mu_{1}).

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}\\
X_{2}\end{pmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{11}&\nu_{12}\\
\nu_{12}&\nu_{22}\end{pmatrix}\right).

(71)

Using Lemma
B.7
, we have

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2};\nu_{11},\nu_{22},\nu_{12})

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}\expect\sigma^{\prime}(X_{1})

(72)

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}\expect\cos(X_{1})

(73)

\displaystyle=\nu_{12}e^{-\nu_{11}/2}\cos(\mu_{1})

(74)

by (
65
).
∎

Appendix H
Theoretical Guarantees

The objective of this section is to provide a theoretical analysis of the dissimilarity between
Y_{0}
and
Y
.
We recall the layer-by-layer Normal approximation resulting in
Y_{\text{ana}}
(henceforth,
Y
) alongside the exact neural network formula resulting in
Y_{0}
.

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=Y^{\ell}

\displaystyle Y_{0}

\displaystyle=Y^{\ell}_{0}

(75a)

\displaystyle Y^{k}

\displaystyle=\normal g^{k}(Y^{k-1}),

\displaystyle Y_{0}^{k}

\displaystyle=g^{k}(Y_{0}^{k-1}),

\displaystyle k\in\mathinner{\left\{1\ldots\ell\right\}},

(75b)

\displaystyle Y^{0}

\displaystyle=X

\displaystyle Y_{0}^{0}

\displaystyle=X,

(75c)

where
g^{k}
is the function

\displaystyle g^{k}(x)

\displaystyle=g(x;A^{k},b^{k},C^{k},d^{k}).

(75d)

We will use the Wasserstein distance to measure the distance between
Y
and
Y_{0}
.

The ultimate goal is to show that
d_{\text{W}}(Y_{0},Y)
is small.
We will build up this quantity by recursion through the layers of the neural network.
The key idea is that the error induced by Normal approximation gets worse with every subsequent layer of the network.

H.1
Recursive triangle inequality

Our basic triangle inequality is

\displaystyle\Delta^{k}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}=d_{\mathrm{W}}(Y_{0}^{k},Y^{k})

(76)

\displaystyle=d_{\mathrm{W}}(g^{k}(Y_{0}^{k-1}),Y^{k})

(definition of hidden layer (
75b
))

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{W}}(g^{k}(Y_{0}^{k-1}),g^{k}(Y^{k-1}))+d_{\mathrm{W}}(g^{k}(Y^{k-1}),Y^{k})

(triangle inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla g^{k}\right\|_{\infty}d_{\mathrm{W}}(Y_{0}^{k-1},Y^{k-1})+d_{\mathrm{W}}(g^{k}(Y^{k-1}),Y^{k})

(Lipschitz property of
d_{\mathrm{W}}
)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla g^{k}\right\|_{\infty}\Delta^{k-1}+d_{\mathrm{W}}(g^{k}(Y^{k-1}),Y^{k})

(77)

If
X
is Gaussian, then
\Delta^{0}=0
.
We need to fill two blanks to make this formula concrete.

H.2
Lipschitz constant

First, the Lipschitz constant
\left\|\nabla g^{k}\right\|_{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\nabla g(x;A,b,C,d)

\displaystyle=\nabla\mathinner{\left[\sigma(Ax+b)+Cx+d\right]}

(78)

\displaystyle=A^{\intercal}\operatorname{diag}\mathinner{\left\{\sigma^{\prime}(Ax+b)\right\}}+C^{\intercal}

(79)

To bound this quantity,

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla g(x;A,b,C,d)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x}\left\|A^{\intercal}\operatorname{diag}\mathinner{\left\{\sigma^{\prime}(Ax+b)\right\}}+C^{\intercal}\right\|

(80)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\sigma^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|A\right\|+\left\|C\right\|

(81)

H.3
Non-normality

Second, we need to bound the distance between
g^{k}(Y^{k-1})
and
Y^{k}
.
The importance of the recursion (
77
) is that at layer
k
, we assess the goodness of fit between
g^{k}(Y^{k-1})
, a nonlinear transformation of a Normal random vector, and
Y^{k}
, its approximant.
As the input to
\sigma
is Normal,
the non-normality of
g^{k}(Y^{k-1})
depends on the non-linearity of
g^{k}
, as measured by its second derivatives:

d_{\mathrm{W}}\mathinner{\left(g^{k}(Y^{k-1}),Y^{k}\right)}\leq\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\left\|\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\right\|\left\|\Sigma_{k}\right\|^{1/2}\left\|A^{k}\Sigma_{k-1}\mathinner{\left(A^{k}\right)}^{\intercal}\right\|^{3/2}\\
\cdot\mathinner{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{d}^{k}}\expect\left\|\sigma^{\prime\prime}(Y^{k-1}_{i})\right\|^{4}\right)}^{1/4}\mathinner{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{d}^{k}}\expect\left\|\sigma^{\prime}(Y^{k-1}_{i})\right\|^{4}\right)}^{1/4}

(82)

where
\mathsf{d}^{k}
is the number of neurons in layer
k
and
\Sigma^{k}=\Cov Y^{k}
.
As a cruder approximation,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{W}}\mathinner{\left(g^{k}(Y^{k-1}),Y^{k}\right)}\leq\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\mathinner{\left(\mathsf{d}^{k}\right)}^{1/2}\left\|\sigma^{\prime\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\sigma^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\Sigma_{k}^{-1}\right\|\left\|\Sigma_{k}\right\|^{1/2}\left\|A^{k}\Sigma_{k-1}\mathinner{\left(A^{k}\right)}^{\intercal}\right\|^{3/2}

(83)

These inequalities are applications of Lem.
H.2
, which follows from a result from the functional analysis of Gaussian spaces:

Theorem H.1

(
34
)
.

Fix
d\geq 2
, and let
C=\{C(i,j);i,j=1,\dots,d\}
be a
d\times d
positive definite matrix. Suppose that
F=(F_{1},\dots,F_{d})
is a
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random vector such that
\expect F_{i}=0
and
F_{i}\in\mathbb{D}^{2,4}
for every
i=1,\dots,d
. Assume moreover that
F
has covariance matrix
C
. Then

d_{W}(F,\mathcal{N}_{d}(0,C))\leq\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\|C^{-1}\|\|C\|^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\expect\|D^{2}F_{i}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{4}\right)^{1/4}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\expect\|DF_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{h}}^{4}\right)^{1/4},

where
\mathcal{N}_{d}(0,C)
indicates a
d
-dimensional centered Gaussian vector, with covariance matrix equal to
C
,
and
D
is the Malliavin derivative with respect to the underlying isonormal process.

This theorem is stated in terms of the deep generality of the Malliavin calculus for nonlinear functionals
F
of infinite-dimensional Gaussian processes.
We adapt it to an inequality for finite-dimensional Gaussian
X
:

Lemma H.2

(Second-order Poincaré inequality)
.

Let
X\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{X},\Sigma_{X})
be a
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued random vector and
Y=f(X)
be a
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random vector with mean
\mu
and covariance matrix
\Sigma_{Y}
.
Then

d_{\mathrm{W}}\mathinner{\left(Y,\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma_{Y})\right)}\leq\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\|\|\Sigma_{Y}\|^{1/2}\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|^{3/2}\cdot\mathinner{\left[\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathinner{\left(\expect\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{i}(X)\right\|^{4}\right)}^{1/4}\right]}\mathinner{\left[\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathinner{\left(\expect\left\|\nabla f_{i}(X)\right\|^{4}\right)}^{1/4}\right]},

where
\nabla^{2}f_{i}(X)
is the Hessian of
f_{i}
evaluated at
X
, and
\nabla f_{i}(X)
is the gradient of
f_{i}
evaluated at
X
.

While this lemma is technically a basic application of Thm.
H.1
, some nontrivial prerequisites are required to get it right.
4
4

4

cf.
27
which does not correctly scale with the input covariance.

Before proving this lemma, we first provide an exposition of isonormal Gaussian processes and the spaces
\mathbb{D}^{m,p}
.
This material is derived from
36
;
35
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an abstract index set.
A Gaussian process
\{X(i)\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is a family of
\mathbb{R}
-valued random variables
such that for every finite subset
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{I}
,
the random variables
\{X(i)\}_{i\in\mathcal{J}}
are jointly Normal.
For example: let
Z
be a multivariate Normal random vector taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let
\mathcal{I}=\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Define
X(i)=Z_{i}
for
i\in\mathcal{I}
.
Then
\{X(i)\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is a Gaussian process.
Another example:
in problems of learning a function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
from data,
the function of interest is modeled as a realization of a Gaussian process
\{f(x)\}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}
with a covariance kernel
k(x,x^{\prime})

(
46
;
43
)
.

The first example above had a finite index set. The second had a finite-dimensional index set.
But the generality of Thm.
H.1
is amenable to an infinite-dimensional index set such as that obtained by a suitable restriction of
L^{2}
.
For example, the modern theory of weak convergence is interested in identifying so-called Donsker classes of functions
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for a sequence of i.i.d.
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-valued random variables
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
,

\displaystyle G(h)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\left(h(X_{i})-\expect h(X)\right)}

converges in distribution to a Gaussian process indexed by
h

(
50
)
.

Prepared with the vocabulary of abstract Gaussian processes, we introduce isonormal Gaussian processes:

Definition H.3

(Isonormal Gaussian process)
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a separable Hilbert space. A stochastic process
W=\{W(h),h\in\mathfrak{h}\}
is an isonormal Gaussian process if it satisfies the following two properties:

1.

For every
h\in\mathfrak{h}
,
\expect W(h)=0
.

2.

For every
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}
,
\expect W(h_{1})W(h_{2})=\left\langle h_{1},h_{2}\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}
.

Let
\{W(h)\}_{h\in\mathfrak{h}}
be an isonormal Gaussian process.
Let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of all random variables that depend “nicely” on a finite subset of
W
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\mathinner{\left\{f(W(h_{1}),\ldots,W(h_{n}))\mid f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}\in\mathfrak{h}\right\}}

where
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the space of smooth functions with compact support.
The Malliavin derivative is defined by

\displaystyle Df(W(h_{1}),\ldots,W(h_{n}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{i}f(W(h_{1}),\ldots,W(h_{n}))h_{i}.

The
m
th Malliavin derivative, which takes values in
\mathfrak{h}^{\otimes m}
, the
m
th tensor power of
\mathfrak{h}
, is defined iteratively.
For
m\geq 1
and
p\geq 1
, define the space
\mathbb{D}^{m,p}
to be the closure of
\mathcal{S}
under the norm
5
5

5

Compare to the Sobolev space
W^{m,p}
.

\displaystyle\|F\|_{\mathbb{D}^{m,p}}^{p}

\displaystyle=\expect\left|F\right|^{p}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\expect\left\|D^{i}F\right\|_{\mathfrak{h}^{\otimes i}}^{p}.

Proof of Lemma
H.2
.

In order to apply Thm.
H.1
, we need to represent
Y=f(X)
as a member of
\mathbb{D}^{2,4}
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\expect X=0
.
Next, we represent
X
in terms of an isonormal Gaussian process.
Let
\mathfrak{h}
be the Hilbert space
\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with the standard dot product.
Let
\xi
be a standard Normal random vector taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Define
Z(h)=\left\langle h,\xi\right\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}
for
h\in\mathfrak{h}
.
Then
\{Z(h)\}_{h\in\mathfrak{h}}
is an isonormal Gaussian process and
\xi_{i}=Z(e_{i})
for
i=1,\ldots,n
, where
e_{i}
is the
i
th standard basis vector.
Next, we note that

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\xi

\displaystyle\overset{\mathcal{D}}{=}\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}e_{i}.

Therefore, by approximating
f
using smooth bounded functions, we can write

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=f(X)

\displaystyle=f\mathinner{\left(\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}e_{j}\right)}

The Malliavin derivative of
Y
is given by

\displaystyle DY_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}f_{k}\mathinner{\left(\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}e_{j}\right)}e_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\left\{Df_{k}\mathinner{\left(\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}e_{j}\right)}\mathinner{\left[\mathinner{\dfrac{\partial{}}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}\mathinner{\left(\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}e_{j}\right)}\right]}\right\}}e_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathinner{\left\{Df_{k}\mathinner{\left(\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}e_{j}\right)}\mathinner{\left[\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}e_{i}\right]}\right\}}e_{i}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\nabla f_{k}(X),

and likewise the second Malliavin derivative is (identifying
\mathfrak{h}\otimes\mathfrak{h}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
)

\displaystyle D^{2}Y_{k}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}\nabla^{2}f_{k}(X)\Sigma_{X}^{1/2}.

Thus the conclusion of Thm.
H.1
becomes:

d_{W}(Y,\mathcal{N}_{d}(\mu_{Y},\Sigma_{T}))\leq\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\|\Sigma_{Y}^{-1}\|\|\Sigma_{Y}\|^{1/2}\\
\cdot\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\expect\|D^{2}Y_{i}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{4}\right)^{1/4}}_{\mathclap{\leq\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|^{2/2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\expect\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{i}(X)\right\|^{4}\right)^{1/4}}}\\
\cdot\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\expect\|DY_{i}\|_{\mathfrak{h}}^{4}\right)^{1/4}}_{\mathclap{\leq\left\|\Sigma_{X}\right\|^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\expect\left\|\nabla f_{i}(X)\right\|^{4}\right)^{1/4}}}.

∎

Appendix I
Supplement to §
5.2

A neural mapping
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
and a variance
\sigma^{2}
are chosen to maximize the log-likelihood of the generative model

\displaystyle y\mid x\sim\mathcal{N}(f(x),\sigma^{2})

(84)

for a set of inputs
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
and outputs
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
.
At inference time, the input
x
is corrupted to
\hat{X}=x+\epsilon
, where
\epsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\epsilon})
is a perturbation with a known distribution.
We make the prediction

\displaystyle\hat{Y}\mid\hat{X}

\displaystyle=f(\hat{X})+\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})

(85)

which we approximate by

\displaystyle\hat{Y}\mid\hat{X}

\displaystyle\approx\mathcal{N}(\expect(f(x)\mid\hat{X}),\sigma^{2}+\Var(f(x)\mid\hat{X})).

(86)

We apply this to the California housing dataset consisting of roughly 20,000
(x,y)
pairs, in which
y
is the log median house price of a block from the 1990 United States Census, and
x
is a vector of eight features of that block consisting of latitude, longitude, and other demographic information.
At inference time, we corrupt the input
x
with Gaussian noise.

I.1
Data

The California Housing dataset
(
28
)
consists of 20460 census blocks from the 1990 United States Census.
We split this dataset into roughly 70% training, 10% validation, and 20% test.
We pre-processed this dataset by log-transforming some of the variables and standardizing them using the means and standard deviations of the training set.
The regressors are:

•

latitude of the block

•

longitude of the block

•

median income of the block

•

(logarithm) average number of rooms per household

•

(logarithm) average number of bedrooms per household

•

(logarithm) population of the block

•

(logarithm) number of households in the block

The predictor is the log median house price of the block.

I.2
Network

We used a single-hidden-layer neural network to parameterize a model consisting of a superposition of a linear term and a sinusoidal term:

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=C\sin(Ax+b)+C^{\prime}x+d,

(87)

implemented using two layers:

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}C\\
C^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}f^{1}(x)+d,

(88)

\displaystyle f^{1}(x)

\displaystyle=\sin\mathinner{\left(\begin{pmatrix}A\\
0\end{pmatrix}x+\begin{pmatrix}b\\
0\end{pmatrix}\right)}+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}x+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},

(89)

The number of hidden neurons (the number of rows of
A
) is 7.
This number was the result of hand-tuning to reduce the subjective appearance of overfitting in the validation set.

I.3
Training

We initialized
A
with i.i.d. Gaussian entries having mean zero and variance equal to the reciprocal of the number of columns.
We initialized
b
with i.i.d.
\operatorname{Uniform}(-\pi,\pi)
.
We initialized
C
,
C^{\prime}
, and
d
to zero.

We trained this network using the Adam optimizer on Optax
(
10
)
with learning rate
10^{-1}
for the first
2000
steps and
10^{-2}
for
6000
steps.

I.4
Inference

At inference time, we corrupt the input with Gaussian noise with zero variance in the latitude and longitude features, and 10X population covariance in the other six features.
The two sources of uncertainty in the Monte Carlo simulation are the randomness of the train/val/test split (as judged relative to a hypothetical population) and the test data augmentation.
In order to report a standard error that lumps both sources of uncertainty, we obtain 100 bootstrap samples of the test set.
Within each bootstrap sample, we corrupt each input with 100 realizations of noise.

Appendix J
Supplement to §
5.3

A neural mapping
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is trained to maximize the log-likelihood of the generative model

\displaystyle y\mid x

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(p)

(90)

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Phi(f(x))

(91)

for a set of inputs
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
and outputs
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
.

At inference time, the input
x
is corrupted to
z=Px
, where
P
is a wide measurement matrix.
The situation in which
P
is rank deficient corresponds to missing features.
In order to use the model (
90
) for inference, we impute the missing features using linear regression:

\displaystyle\hat{X}\mid z

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\hat{\mu},\hat{\Sigma}),

(92a)

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}

\displaystyle=\mu+\Sigma P^{\intercal}\mathinner{\left(P\Sigma P^{\intercal}\right)}^{-1}(z-\mu),

(92b)

\displaystyle\hat{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\Sigma-\mathinner{\left(\Sigma P^{\intercal}\right)}\mathinner{\left(P\Sigma P^{\intercal}\right)}^{-1}\mathinner{\left(P\Sigma\right)}.

(92c)

Here,
\mu
and
\Sigma
are the population mean and variance-covariance matrix of
x
, estimated from the training data.

Afterwards we make the uncertainty-aware prediction

\displaystyle\hat{Y}\mid\hat{X}

\displaystyle\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(\hat{p})

(93)

\displaystyle\hat{p}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\expect\mathinner{\left[\Phi(f(x))\mid\hat{X}\right]}.

(94)

At inference time, we drop all features except “Operating Gross Margin” and impute the rest of the balance sheet and cash flow features using linear regression following (
92
).

J.1
Data

The Taiwanese bankruptcy dataset
(
29
)
consists of 6819 instances of Taiwanese companies from 1999 to 2009.
There are 95 features consisting of balance sheet (assets and liabilities) and cash flow information.
The target variable is a binary indicator of bankruptcy.
We preprocessed this dataset by standardizing the features using the means and standard deviations of the training set.
We split this dataset into roughly 70% training, 10% validation, and 20% test.

J.2
Network

We used a single-hidden-layer neural network with a width of 200, depth of 3, and sine activation function:

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=C\sin(Ax+b)+d

(95)

where
A
is a
200\times 95
matrix,
b
is a
200\times 1
vector,
C
is a
1\times 200
vector, and
d
is a
1\times 1
vector.
We initialized
A
with i.i.d. Gaussian entries having mean zero and variance equal to the reciprocal of the number of columns.
We initialized
b
with i.i.d.
\operatorname{Uniform}(-\pi,\pi)
.
We initialized
C
to zero and
d=\Phi^{-1}(\expect_{\text{train}}y)
.

J.3
Training

We trained this network using the AdamW optimizer
(
31
;
10
)
with learning rate
10^{-5}
for
5000
steps.

J.4
Reporting

We report the average log predicted probability (higher is better) of the correct label on test data in the Taiwanese bankruptcy dataset.
We also report the standard error of the mean.

:
Receiver operator characteristic curve of test data in the Taiwanese bankruptcy dataset based on varying the threshold for
\hat{p}
.

Appendix K
Supplement to §
5.4

We expand on each of the terms in the evidence lower bound (
2
), repeated below.

\displaystyle\theta^{*}=\arg\min_{\theta}\mathinner{\left\{\expect_{w\sim q(w;\theta)}\log p(y\mid w,x)+D_{\text{KL},w}(q(w;\theta)\mid p(w))\right\}}.

Our neural network
f(x;w)
consists of a single hidden layer.
The weights
w
encompass:

•

A
,
b
: pre-activation weights,

•

C
,
d
: post-activation weights, as well as

•

\lambda
: (homoscedastic) output log precision.

The log likelihood of
w
is Normal:

\displaystyle\log p(y\mid w,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathinner{\left(-\lambda+e^{\lambda}\mathinner{\left[y-f(x;w)\right]}^{2}\right)}+\frac{1}{2}\log\mathinner{\left[2\pi\right]}.

The prior distribution
p(w)
is the Kaiming initialization of independent Normal distributions, zero mean in each matrix entry and variance equal to
2/\text{fan-in}
.
Like
55
;
40
;
54
, the variational distribution
q(w;\theta)
is a Gaussian matrix with independent entries, parameterized by means and log precisions.

We train using the Adam optimizer with a learning rate of 0.1 for 5 000 epochs.
Each epoch draws 10 random samples from the variational distribution
q(w;\theta)
per training instance and uses them to compute the gradient of the ELBO.

Each network takes about 2 minutes on a Nvidia T1200 GPU.

In reporting, we draw one million random variates from the variational posterior distribution for up to 100 instances from the test set.

The datasets are

•

Combined cycle power plant
(
41
)

•

Concrete compressive strength
(
19
)

•

Energy efficiency (we predict the heating load)
(
4
)

•

Wine quality
(
39
)

Appendix L
Supplement to §
5.5

The random variable
\bm{1}_{U<\Phi(x)}
has a Bernoulli distribution with parameter
\Phi(x)
,
which contributes an additional diagonal variance term to Lemma
2.4
as we see from applying the Law of Total Covariance:

\mathinner{\left(\Cov g_{\tilde{\sigma}}(X;A,b,C,d)\right)}_{i,j}=\mathinner{\left(\Cov\expect\mathinner{\left[g_{\tilde{\sigma}}(X;A,b,C,d)\mid X\right]}\right)}_{i,j}\\
+\mathinner{\left(\expect\Cov\mathinner{\left[g_{\tilde{\sigma}}(X;A,b,C,d)\mid X\right]}\right)}_{i,j}

(96)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left(\Cov\expect\mathinner{\left[g_{\tilde{\sigma}}(X;A,b,C,d)\mid X\right]}\right)}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\mathinner{\left(\Cov g_{\sigma}(X;A,b,C,d)\right)}_{i,j}

(97)

since
\expect\mathinner{\left(\tilde{\sigma}(X,U)\mid X\right)}=\sigma(X)
and

\displaystyle\mathinner{\left(\expect\Cov\mathinner{\left[g_{\tilde{\sigma}}(X;A,b,C,d)\mid X\right]}\right)}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=4\delta_{ij}\expect\Var\mathinner{\left[\mathinner{\left(g_{\tilde{\sigma}}(X;A,b,C,d)\right)}_{i}\mid X\right]}

(98)

\displaystyle=4\delta_{ij}\expect\Phi(\xi_{i})\mathinner{\left(1-\Phi(\xi_{i})\right)}

(99)

where
\xi_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\nu_{ii})
.
Using Owen’s T function
(
37
)
, we have

\displaystyle\expect\Phi(\xi_{i})\mathinner{\left(1-\Phi(\xi_{i})\right)}

\displaystyle=2T\mathinner{\left(\frac{\mu_{i}}{\sqrt{1+\nu_{ii}}},\frac{\nu_{ii}}{1+2\nu_{ii}}\right)}.

(100)

Appendix M
Supplement to §
5.1

We apply our method and other benchmarks to 38 different ensembles of random neural networks:

•

network architecture
\in\{\text{wide},\text{deep}\}

•

weights
\in\{\text{initialized},\text{trained}\}

•

activation function
\in\{\text{probit},\text{probit residual},\text{sine},\text{sine residual}\}

(There are no trained networks containing Heaviside or Heaviside-residual layers.)
From each ensemble, we sample one neural network and evaluate the goodness of approximation of the output distribution for input distributions:

•

variance
\in\{\text{small},\text{medium},\text{large}\}
.

In each case we compare the distributions of:

•

Y_{0}
, the true distribution

•

Y_{1}
, the pseudo-true Gaussian distribution

•

Y_{\mathrm{ana}}
, the analytic layer-wise Gaussian approximation (our method)

•

Y_{\mathrm{mfa}}
, mean-field: applying our method for moment propagation and setting off-diagonal layer covariances to zero

•

Y_{\mathrm{lin}}
, linearization-based moment propagation

•

Y_{\mathrm{u^{\prime}95}}
, unscented transform of the whole network using
\kappa=2

•

Y_{\mathrm{u^{\prime}02}}
, unscented transform of the whole network using
\alpha=0.001,\beta=2,\kappa=2

Ensembles of neural networks

This section specifies the 38 ensembles of random neural networks and the three ensembles of inputs used to produce the
114
test cases that follow.
The neural networks in this example are parameterized as in Def.
2.1
.
Let
d_{\mathrm{hidden}}
and
w_{\mathrm{hidden}}
be the depth and width of the hidden layers, respectively.
A random neural network
f\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
is specified with
d_{\mathrm{hidden}}+1
layers.
The output layer is linear, with weights
C^{d_{\mathrm{hidden}}+1}
and biases
d^{d_{\mathrm{hidden}}+1}
.
The first hidden layer has
C^{1}=0_{w_{\mathrm{hidden}}\times 1}
,
d^{1}=0_{w_{\mathrm{hidden}}\times 1}
.
The four degrees of freedom in the test cases are:

architecture

•

if architecture = wide, then
d_{\mathrm{hidden}}=5
,
w_{\mathrm{hidden}}=400
;

•

if architecture = deep, then
d_{\mathrm{hidden}}=20
,
w_{\mathrm{hidden}}=100
.

weights

•

if weights = initialized:

–

A
matrices are initialized with i.i.d. Gaussian entries having mean zero and variance equal to the reciprocal of the number of columns times
\sqrt{2}

–

if
\text{activation}\in\{\text{probit},\text{probit residual}\}
, then
b
vectors are initialized with indepedently sampled entries from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)

–

if
\text{activation}\in\{\text{sine},\text{sine residual}\}
, then
b
vectors are initialized with indepedently sampled entries from
\mathcal{U}(-\pi,\pi)

–

if
\text{activation}\in\{\text{probit},\text{sine}\}
, then
C
matrices are initialized to the zero matrix.

–

if
\text{activation}\in\{\text{probit residual},\text{sine residual}\}
, then square
C
matrices are initialized to the identity matrix, and all other
C
matrices are initialized to the zero matrix.

–

d
vectors are initialized to the zero vector.

•

if weights = trained and
\text{activation}\not\in\{\text{Heaviside},\text{Heaviside residual}\}
, the following initialization as above, the neural network is trained to minimize the mean squared error loss on a pseudorandomly generated dataset of ten
(x,y)
samples drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
Training consists of using the AdamW optimizer
(
31
)
with a learning rate of
10^{-6}
for 30,000 iterations and until the loss is less than
10^{-8}
(whichever is later).
Implementation due to Optax,
10

activation function

•

if
\text{activation}\in\{\text{probit},\text{probit residual}\}
, then
\sigma(x)=2\Phi(x)-1
where
\Phi
is the cumulative distribution function of the standard normal distribution

•

if
\text{activation}\in\{\text{sine},\text{sine residual}\}
, then
\sigma(x)=\sin(x)

•

if
\text{activation}\in\{\text{GeLU},\text{GeLU residual}\}
, then
\sigma(x)=x\Phi(x)
.

•

if
\text{activation}\in\{\text{ReLU},\text{ReLU residual}\}
, then
\sigma(x)=\max(0,x)
.

•

if
\text{activation}\in\{\text{Heaviside},\text{Heaviside residual}\}
, then
\sigma(x)=\bm{1}_{\{x\geq 0\}}

variance

•

if variance = small, then
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,10^{-2}I)

•

if variance = medium, then
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)

•

if variance = large, then
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,10^{2}I)

Simulation and reporting

The only source of uncertainty in our numerical results is the “true distribution” of
Y_{0}=f(X)
.
For this we use twenty independent realizations of
N=2^{16}
quasi-Monte Carlo samples
(
51
)
.
We report statistical uncertainty in the form of
\text{mean}\pm\text{standard error}
within the independent realizations.
The distributional uncertainty is too small to visualize in figures, so we plot the pooled data without an uncertainty indication.

The Wasserstein distance between a Normal distribution and
Y_{0}
is computed by

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{W}}(\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}),Y_{0})

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|y_{(i)}-Q\mathinner{\left(\frac{i-1/2}{N}\right)}\right|

where
\{y_{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are the quasi-Monte Carlo samples of
Y_{0}
sorted in ascending order, and
Q
is the quantile function of
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
.
When plotting distributions, we show
Y_{0}
using a histogram with 50 bins.

The horizontal axes are scaled to include the 0.5th (99.5th) percentiles of the quasi-Monte Carlo samples of
Y_{0}
, or the mean minus (plus) three standard deviations of
Y_{1}
, whichever is smaller (greater).

The entire suite of 72 test cases takes (including initialization, training, quasi-Monte Carlo, and reporting)
18 minutes
on an Ubuntu system with a 11th Gen Intel® Core™ i7-11850H CPU and 32GB of RAM.

Appendix N
Comments on computational complexity

The cost of moment matching, like that of linearized covariance propagation, is cubic in the number of hidden neurons due to the need to evaluate “sandwich” covariances such as
A\Sigma A^{\intercal}
.

6
6

6

Contrary to occasional claims that the cost of propagating a covariance matrix scales
quadratically
with the number of hidden neurons
(
2
, §5)
.

By contrast, the computational complexity of evaluating a neural network is quadratic in the number of hidden neurons.
Thus, theoretically, moment matching is one polynomial order of magnitude slower than point prediction, on the same order as linearization.
For neural networks with high input dimension, moment matching is much faster than Monte Carlo, which scales exponentially with dimension.

We performed a simple experiment using Jupyter Notebook’s
%%timeit
to compare the computational cost of moment matching and Monte Carlo.
We compare our method with the ground truth method used in §
5.1
, which runs quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) on
2^{16}
samples.
We use GeLU because it has the most complicated covariance expression (see Appendix
D
).

On “wide residual” networks (defined in Appendix
M
):

•

Our method takes 88.4 ms ± 5.96 ms.

•

QMC takes 1.79 s ± 83 ms.

On “deep residual” networks (defined in Appendix
M
):

•

Our method takes 23.7 ms ± 957
\mu
s.

•

QMC takes 878 ms ± 61.8 ms.
